It’s over stuff that other people would probably just fix and move on from, but I can’t stop feeling like I ruined it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about nine months, I think. It wasn’t as obvious at first, but once school and work started slipping, it got a lot harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I still do some things if I have to, but I don’t get much out of them. I’ll walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore texts from the volunteer group. Those used to be the things that felt like me, and now I just don’t really feel anything about them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it nervous. I’m definitely not relaxed, but it feels more like being worn down or stuck in my head than on edge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s over stuff that other people would probably just fix and move on from, but I can’t stop feeling like I ruined it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about nine months, I think. It wasn’t as obvious at first, but once school and work started slipping, it got a lot harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I still do some things if I have to, but I don’t get much out of them. I’ll walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore texts from the volunteer group. Those used to be the things that felt like me, and now I just don’t really feel anything about them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it nervous. I’m definitely not relaxed, but it feels more like being worn down or stuck in my head than on edge.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a big change. My clothes seem to fit about the same, so I don’t think my weight has changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything, but sometimes when I’m driving home after class, or lying awake at night, I think it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressful events or changes in your life around the time these feelings started, about nine months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started after I took on the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship. It’s been going on for about five months now, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last nine months where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good. Like I felt like Miami was mine, if that makes sense. But I was also getting irritated really fast—if a coworker made some little comment or slowed me down, I’d snap way harder than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you still enjoy the activities you used to, like sketching or volleyball?\n\nPatient: Not really. Lately I can walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and just ignore the volunteer group texts, and I don’t really feel much about it. Those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Lately I can walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and just ignore the volunteer group texts, and I don’t really feel much about it. Those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you enjoy anything at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much. I’d feel like I had this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. It felt like I had to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did that happen suddenly, like switching from one day to the next?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. It didn’t feel like a clean switch, like I woke up one day as a different person. It wasn’t as obvious at first, but once school and work started slipping, it got a lot harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I still do some things if I have to, but I don’t get much out of them. I’ll walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore texts from the volunteer group. I’m definitely not relaxed, but it feels more like being worn down or stuck in my head than on edge.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a big change. It felt like I had to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did that happen suddenly, like switching from one day to the next?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. It didn’t feel like a clean switch, like I woke up one day as a different person. It was more like I’d notice after a bit that I was moving faster and feeling unusually up.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did those periods last for several days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s been kind of a pattern for about five months now, since I started the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship. I haven’t paid close enough attention to say if it cuts off all at once or fades out. I usually just notice afterward that I’m not feeling as revved up anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did you ever do things that you normally wouldn't do, like spending a lot of money or taking risks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear example of spending a lot or doing something risky that I normally wouldn’t. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Did you spend more money than usual during those weeks when you felt almost too good?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern of spending more during those weeks. It wasn’t as obvious at first, but once school and work started slipping, it got a lot harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I still do some things if I have to, but I don’t get much out of them. I’ll walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore texts from the volunteer group. Those used to be the things that felt like me, and now I just don’t really feel anything about them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it nervous. I’m definitely not relaxed, but it feels more like being worn down or stuck in my head than on edge.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a big change. My clothes seem to fit about the same, so I don’t think my weight has changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything, but sometimes when I’m driving home after class, or lying awake at night, I think it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressful events or changes in your life around the time these feelings started, about nine months ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started after I took on the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship. It’s been going on for about five months now, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last nine months where you felt significantly better for at least a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good. Like I felt like Miami was mine, if that makes sense. But I was also getting irritated really fast—if a coworker made some little comment or slowed me down, I’d snap way harder than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you still enjoy the activities you used to, like sketching or volleyball?\n\nPatient: Not really. Lately I can walk right past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and just ignore the volunteer group texts, and I don’t really feel much about it. Those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you enjoy anything at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much. Even during those “better” weeks, stuff that usually feels good to me didn’t really hit the same. I could walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, ignore the volunteer texts… and I just didn’t feel much about it. Those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you ever actually feel happy or carefree?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I wouldn’t say carefree exactly, but there were weeks where I felt almost too good—like everything was mine, like Miami was mine. But it came with me getting irritated really fast too, like if a coworker slowed me down or made some little comment, I’d snap way harder than normal.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice any increase in your energy levels or activity during those weeks when you felt almost too good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’d feel like I had this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. Like I’d work a full day on pipes, and instead of going home and sitting down, I’d go to evening class, reorganize all my tools, clean my apartment, then start drawing out systems. It felt like I had to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did that happen suddenly, like switching from one day to the next?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. It didn’t feel like a clean switch, like I woke up one day as a different person. It was more like I’d notice after a bit that I was moving faster and feeling unusually up.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did those periods last for several days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s been kind of a pattern for about five months now, since I started the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship. I can’t always pin it down day by day, but it’s not like it’s just one afternoon and gone.\n\nDoctor: When you feel almost too good, does it usually end abruptly or gradually?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close enough attention to say if it cuts off all at once or fades out. I usually just notice afterward that I’m not feeling as revved up anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did you ever do things that you normally wouldn't do, like spending a lot of money or taking risks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear example of spending a lot or doing something risky that I normally wouldn’t. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Did you spend more money than usual during those weeks when you felt almost too good?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern of spending more during those weeks. Nothing stands out to me like a big purchase or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you felt almost too good, did it feel like a distinct episode or period, separate from your normal mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it did feel like a separate stretch of time. There were weeks where I felt almost too good, like everything was kind of mine to take on, and then little things at work—like a coworker slowing me down or making a comment—would set me off way more than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
